            .filter(identity => identity.type === 'AudienceSegmentsUser')
            .map(identity => identity.name)
            .filter(name => name);

        googletag.pubads()
            .setTargeting('property', 'value-of-property')    // Change and duplicate this line for as many additional keywords to send
            .setTargeting('doi', gptClean('10.1002/smll.202511946')) // Publication DOI
            .setTargeting('journalDoi', gptClean('10.1002/(ISSN)1613-6829')) // journal DOI
            .setTargeting('journalCode', gptClean('16136829')) // journal code
            .setTargeting('pbPage', gptClean(gptPage))
            .setTargeting('pbSubPage', gptClean(gptSubPage))
            .setTargeting('AudienceSegmentsUser', segments)
            .setTargeting('urlpath', gptClean(document.location.pathname))
			.setTargeting('env', window.adobeDataLayer?.[0]?.site?.environment || 'n/a');


        googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-leaderboard')
            .defineSizeMapping(sizes.leaderboard)
            .addService(googletag.pubads())
            .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);

        if(window.innerWidth >= 992) { // Remove MPU ad units from mobile view (LIT-621710)
            googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-rail-1')
                .defineSizeMapping(sizes.rail)
                .setTargeting('Position', 'advert-rail-1')
                .addService(googletag.pubads())
                .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);

            googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-rail-2')
                .defineSizeMapping(sizes.rail)
                .setTargeting('Position', 'advert-rail-2')
                .addService(googletag.pubads())
                .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);
        }

		onDvtagReady();	// DoubleVerify (PERICLES-19717)

        if(isProgrammaticAdsEnabled) {
            // PERICLES-17135
            googletag.pubads().disableInitialLoad();
        }
		
        googletag.enableServices();
    });

    if (isProgrammaticAdsEnabled) {
        googletag.cmd.push(function () {
            apstag.fetchBids({timeout: 2000}, function (bids) {
                apstag.setDisplayBids();
                onDvtagReady(function () {
					setTimeout(() => {
						googletag.pubads().refresh();
					}, 300);
                })
            });
        });
    }

<!-- loading Javascript for using the persistence Service -->
    <script src="/wro/7eefe1ac3fa1fd1db62439ce305b72e4595ffa0f~thiss-ds.js" nonce="9efabd4e366b44af-SJC"></script>

    <script type="text/javascript" nonce="9efabd4e366b44af-SJC">

        function getParameter(parameterName) {
            let result = null,
                tmp = [];
            location.search
                .substr(1)
                .split("&")
                .forEach(function (item) {
                    tmp = item.split("=");
                    if (tmp[0] === parameterName) result = tmp[1];
                });
            return result;
        }

        var SeamlessAccessService = {
            spUrl: "/action/ssostart",
            returnURL: window.location.href,
            my_context: 'seamlessaccess.org',
            queryParamsDelimiter: encodeURIComponent('?'),
            _persistenceService: null,
            _getPersistenceService: function () {
                if (!this._persistenceService) {
                    this._persistenceService = new thiss.PersistenceService('https://service.seamlessaccess.org/ps/');
                }
                return this._persistenceService;
            },

            getWAYFLessUrl: function (entityId) {
                let redirectURL = getParameter('redirectUri');
                if(redirectURL == null){
                    redirectURL = encodeURIComponent(this.returnURL);
                }
                if(redirectURL.indexOf('saml_referrer') < 0){
                    redirectURL += encodeURIComponent(((redirectURL.indexOf(this.queryParamsDelimiter) !== -1) ? '&' : '?') + 'saml_referrer');
                }
                return this.spUrl + "?idp=" + encodeURIComponent(entityId)
                    + "&redirectUri=" + redirectURL;
            },
            retrieveEntity: function () {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.entities(self.my_context)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // get the results
                            var results = res.data;
                            return resolve(results);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            reject('retrieve entity error: ' + err);
                        });
                });
            },
            updateEntity: function (entitydata) {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                var jsonObj = {};
                try {
                    //try to parse it, if it is a json string
                    jsonObj = JSON.parse(entitydata.val());
                } catch (e) {
                    jsonObj = entitydata;
                }
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.update(self.my_context, jsonObj)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // Process the results
                            var ssoInstitutions = res.data;
                            return resolve(ssoInstitutions);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to update the local storage duo to: ' + err);
                            reject(err);
                        });
                })
            },
            removeEntity: function (entityId) {
                var self = this;
                var persistService = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    persistService.remove(self.my_context, entityId)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            return resolve(res);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to remove from local storage duo to: ' + err);
                        });
                })
            },
            getAccessibleEntityStatus: function (doi, entityId, entityTitle) {
                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    $.ajax({
                        type: 'POST',
                        url: '/action/seamlessAccess',
                        accept: {
                            text: "application/json"
                        },
                        data: {doi: doi, entityid: entityId, entityTitle: entityTitle, eventGroupKey: '3a86de0c-c442-4d28-8af5-2cc4e63981c8'}

                    }).done(function (status) {
                        return resolve(status);
                    });
                });
            },
            getEntitiesWithAllInfo: function (doi) {
                let self = this,
                    arrayOfPromises = [];
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    self.retrieveEntity().then(function (res) {
                        let allSsoInstitutions = res;
                        for (let i in allSsoInstitutions) {
                            let ssoInstitution = allSsoInstitutions[i];
                            arrayOfPromises.push(new Promise(function (resolve) {
                                self.getAccessibleEntityStatus(doi, ssoInstitution.entity.entityID, ssoInstitution.entity.title)
                                    .then(function (entityStatus) {
                                        resolve({
                                            entityID: ssoInstitution.entity.entityID,
                                            entityTitle: ssoInstitution.entity.title,
                                            entityStatusObj: entityStatus,
                                            entitySsoUrl: self.getWAYFLessUrl(ssoInstitution.entity.entityID)
                                        });
                                    }, function (err) {
                                        console.log('failed to get the entity status: ' + err);
                                    });
                            }));
                        }
                        Promise.all(arrayOfPromises)
                            .then(function (finalResponse) {
                                resolve(finalResponse);
                            });

                    }).catch(function (err) {
                        reject(err);
                    })
                });
            }
        };

        window.addEventListener('load', function () {
            document.querySelectorAll(".sso-institution").forEach(function (institution) {
                var entityId = institution.getAttribute('data-entityid');
                var entityName = institution.getAttribute('data-name');
                var entitydata = {
                    entityID: entityId,
                    title: entityName
                };
                institution.addEventListener("click", function (e) {
                    e.preventDefault();
                    var self = this;
                    SeamlessAccessService.updateEntity(entitydata).then(function (res) {
                        console.log(res);
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    }).catch(function (err){
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    });
                });
            });
        });

    </script>

        (function() {
            function riskifiedBeaconLoad() {
                var store_domain = 'https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com';
                var session_id = '4D8B1DBDDBD332A3B9A3ADB4F57ABA32';
                var url = ('https:' == document.location.protocol ? 'https://' : 'http://')
                    + "beacon.riskified.com?shop=" + store_domain + "&sid=" + session_id;
                var s = document.createElement('script');
                s.type = 'text/javascript';
                s.async = true;
                s.src = url;
                var x = document.getElementsByTagName('script')[0];
                x.parentNode.insertBefore(s, x);
            }
            if (window.attachEvent)
                window.attachEvent('onload', riskifiedBeaconLoad)
            else
                window.addEventListener('load', riskifiedBeaconLoad, false);
        })();

   <div class="abstract-group ">
      <section class="article-section article-section__abstract" lang="en" data-lang="en" lang-name="English" id="section-1-en">
         <h2 id="d140344245" class="article-section__header section__title main abstractlang_en main">ABSTRACT</h2>
         <div class="article-section__content en main">
            
            <p>mRNA nanotherapeutics hold immense potential for treating a wide range of diseases, but their widespread clinical adoption is limited by current lipid nanoparticle (LNP) delivery platforms, which frequently face challenges such as limited biocompatibility, immunogenic response, insufficient mRNA delivery efficacy and stringent cold-chain requirements. In this study, we systematically screened a 20-member lipid mixture library by substituting ionizable lipids and sterol components to identify formulations with improved physicochemical and biological profiles. A lead candidate combining γ-oryzanol and DLin-KC2-DMA as LNPs, termed OryKL (or KO 12 LNPs), was identified, exhibiting spherical bleb-type and core–shell nanostructures (∼150&nbsp;nm), high mRNA encapsulation, and significantly enhanced in vitro transfection efficiency compared to cholesterol-based controls. Intravenous administration of OryKL delivered Cre recombinase mRNA effectively across multiple organs in Ai9 reporter mice, resulting in distinct cell-level tropism, and no detectable toxicity or inflammation, as confirmed via qPCR, organ histology, hematological assessment and liver function tests. Additionally, OryKL retained transfection potency for at least 60 days in lyophilized form with 20% (w/v) sucrose, supporting ambient-stable storage. These findings establish γ-oryzanol as a promising sterol alternative and position OryKL as a biocompatible, effective, and storage-stable platform for next-generation mRNA therapeutics.</p>
            </div>
      </section>
   </div>
   <div class="pb-dropzone" data-pb-dropzone="below-abstract-group"><!-- Empty dropzone --></div>
   
   <section class="article-section article-section__full">
      <section class="article-section__content" id="smll73095-sec-0010">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="smll73095-sec-0010-title">1 Introduction</h2>
         
         <p>Messenger RNA has emerged as a promising therapeutic approach for a range of diverse and therapeutically challenging diseases, offering advantages such as no risk of genome integration, high yet transient protein expression, and rapid, flexible, and scalable production [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0001" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0001_R_d140344236e533" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 1 - Nature Reviews Drug Discovery">1</a></span>]. However, mRNA is intrinsically unstable under physiological conditions and is prone to rapid degradation by extracellular nucleases; hence, efficient and safe delivery systems are essential to ensure mRNA protection in circulation and its subsequent uptake and translation within target cells [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0002" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0002_R_d140344236e536" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 2 - Journal of Controlled Release">2</a></span>]. Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) have emerged as the most clinically advanced platform for nucleic acid delivery, with nearly 80 LNP-based gene therapy candidates currently undergoing clinical evaluation [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0003" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0003_R_d140344236e539" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 3 - Signal Transduction and Targeted Therapy">3</a></span>]. However, several challenges continue to limit the broader application of mRNA-LNPs in therapeutic settings beyond vaccines. These include immunogenicity, toxicity, storage constraints, and mRNA delivery inefficiencies [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0004" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0004_R_d140344236e542" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4 - Nucleic Acid Therapeutics">4</a></span>]. These barriers reduce therapeutic efficacy and raise safety concerns, particularly in disease contexts where inflammation is already a key pathological feature. To address these limitations, multiple strategies have been explored, including incorporation of auxiliary components, mRNA sequence modification, and manufacturing process improvements [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0005" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0005_R_d140344236e545" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 5 - Advanced Materials">5</a></span>]. Among these, refining the chemical composition of the LNP itself has emerged as a crucial lever for improving performance [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0006" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0006_R_d140344236e549" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6 - Journal of Drug Delivery Science and Technology">6</a></span>]. A deeper understanding of how individual LNP components influence delivery efficiency, biodistribution, and immune response is therefore essential to guide the rational design of next-generation delivery systems.</p>
         
         <p>Current LNPs are composed of four main components: (1) ionizable lipids mediate mRNA encapsulation, endosomal escape, and cellular uptake [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0007" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0007_R_d140344236e555" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 7 - Nature Reviews Materials">7</a></span>]; (2) phospholipids (often referred as helper lipids) stabilize the LNP structure and facilitate endosomal escape [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0007" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0007_R_d140344236e558" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 7 - Nature Reviews Materials">7</a></span>]; (3) cholesterol improves membrane rigidity and contributes to particle integrity [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0008" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0008_R_d140344236e561" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 8 - Journal of Pharmaceutical Sciences">8</a></span>]; and (4) polyethylene glycol (PEG)–lipids maintain colloidal stability and extend circulation time [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0009" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0009_R_d140344236e564" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 9 - Advanced Drug Delivery Reviews">9</a></span>]. Ionizable lipids are designed to remain neutral at physiological pH to minimize systemic toxicity and immune recognition, while becoming positively charged in the acidic endosomal environment to facilitate mRNA release into the cytosol for translation [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0010" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0010_R_d140344236e567" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 10 - Nature Communications">10</a></span>]. Clinically validated examples include DLin-MC3-DMA used in Onpattro, and ALC-0315 and SM-102 utilized in COVID-19 mRNA vaccines by Pfizer and Moderna, respectively [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0011" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0011_R_d140344236e571" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 11 - Advanced Drug Delivery Reviews">11</a></span>]. While ionizable lipids can confer adjuvant-like properties beneficial for vaccination [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0009" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0009_R_d140344236e574" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 9 - Advanced Drug Delivery Reviews">9</a></span>], accumulating evidence indicates that they may also activate innate immune pathways and induce pro-inflammatory cytokine responses [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0012" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0012_R_d140344236e577" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 12 - Veterinary Pathology">12</a>, <a href="#smll73095-bib-0013" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0013_R_d140344236e577" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 13 - Molecular Therapy">13</a></span>], posing a safety concern for therapeutic applications that require immune quiescence, such as chronic inflammatory and cardiovascular diseases [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0014" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0014_R_d140344236e580" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 14 - Journal of Controlled Release">14</a></span>]. Therefore, careful selection and screening of ionizable lipids is vital to balance delivery efficacy with biocompatibility.</p>
         
         <p>Cholesterol is widely used as a neutral helper lipid due to its critical role in membrane stabilization [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0008" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0008_R_d140344236e586" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 8 - Journal of Pharmaceutical Sciences">8</a></span>]. However, increasing evidence indicates that cholesterol-containing LNPs and liposomes can influence immune cell behavior and promote pro-inflammatory signaling, particularly in macrophage-rich environments [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0015" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0015_R_d140344236e589" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 15 - Journal of Lipid Research">15</a>-<a href="#smll73095-bib-0017" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0017_R_d140344236e589" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 17 - ACS Nano">17</a></span>]. These findings have prompted interest in exploring alternative sterols that could retain or improve nanoparticle performance while mitigating adverse immunological effects. Plant-derived sterols such as β-sitosterol and β-stigmasterol have shown promise in enhancing biocompatibility due to their inherent anti-inflammatory properties, improved cellular uptake, and more efficient gene delivery in nanocarrier systems [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0018" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0018_R_d140344236e592" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 18 - Nature Communications">18</a></span>], offering potential advantages in disease settings where inflammation must be tightly controlled.</p>
         
         <p>Another major translational hurdle is the reliance on ultra-low temperatures, driven primarily by the inherent instability of mRNA and LNP formulations [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0019" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0019_R_d140344236e598" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 19 - Bioactive Materials">19</a></span>], increasing logistical complexity and cost and limiting scalable global distribution. Strategies, such as formulation optimization, incorporating cryoprotectants [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0020" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0020_R_d140344236e601" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 20 - Advanced Healthcare Materials">20</a></span>] and freeze-drying (lyophilization) [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0021" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0021_R_d140344236e604" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 21 - Journal of Controlled Release">21</a></span>], have been explored to improve storage stability and enable handling at refrigerated or ambient temperatures. However, most lyophilization studies to date have been performed on cholesterol-containing LNPs, with limited insight into how novel sterol substitutions would influence lyophilization outcomes.</p>
         
         <p>In this context, γ-oryzanol (ORY), a phytosterol mixture derived from rice bran oil, offers a compelling yet underutilized alternative to cholesterol in LNPs. ORY has been associated with antioxidant and anti-inflammatory activity [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0022" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0022_R_d140344236e611" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 22 - Journal of Agricultural and Food Chemistry">22</a></span>] and has demonstrated excellent biocompatibility in various delivery platforms. Moreover, ORY has been successfully incorporated into solid lipid nanoparticles, yielding stable colloidal structures without compromising nanoparticle integrity [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0023" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0023_R_d140344236e614" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 23 - Food Bioscience">23</a></span>]. These characteristics suggest that ORY could suitable as a sterol component in nanocarriers and provide a rationale for evaluating its behavior in mRNA LNP systems. Importantly, ORY differs structurally from previously investigated phytosterols [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0018" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0018_R_d140344236e617" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 18 - Nature Communications">18</a></span>] that typically differ from cholesterol through relatively modest changes in ring saturation or side-chain substitutions. ORY consists of sterols esterified with bulky ferulate moieties, introducing substantial changes in molecular volume and polarity that are expected to fundamentally alter lipid packing within LNPs.</p>
         
         <p>In this study, we report the systematical evaluation of 20 formulations comprising a fixed helper lipid and PEG-lipid, with varying combinations of ionizable lipids and sterols. Our comprehensive, multi-stage screening process included structural characterization, in vitro transfection performance, and biosafety assessments to identify lead candidates. Among these, the formulation combining ORY and DLin-KC2-DMA as LNPs, termed OryKL (or KO 12 LNPs), exhibited superior biocompatibility and transfection efficiency compared to conventional cholesterol-based systems. We further validated OryKL in vivo, demonstrating efficient mRNA delivery, broad biodistribution, and a favorable safety profile in murine models. Importantly, OryKL retained high transfection efficacy after lyophilization and storage for up to two months when formulated with 20% (w/v) sucrose, underscoring their potential to overcome cold chain limitations. Altogether, our findings establish ORY as a promising alternative sterol for next-generation LNPs and position OryKL as a robust and translationally relevant platform for mRNA therapeutics. This study provides critical design principles for advancing the field toward safer, more efficient, and storage-stable nanocarriers.</p>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="smll73095-sec-0020">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="smll73095-sec-0020-title">2 Results and Discussion</h2>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="smll73095-sec-0030">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="smll73095-sec-0030-title">2.1 Pre-Formulation Lipid Screening Reveals Cytocompatibility and Hemocompatibility of γ-Oryzanol Formulations</h3>
            
            <p>Currently, all approved mRNA LNP products contain 1,2-distearoyl-<i>sn-</i>glycero-3-phosphocholine (DSPC) as the helper lipid and cholesterol as the primary sterol, albeit with differing ionizable and PEG-lipid components [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0024" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0024_R_d140344236e638" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 24 - Drug Metabolism and Pharmacokinetics">24</a></span>]. To tailor LNPs for therapeutic applications beyond vaccines, particularly for systemic intravenous administration for chronic disease treatment, a careful redesign of LNP composition is necessary to balance mRNA delivery efficiency, circulation time, and biocompatibility [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0025" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0025_R_d140344236e641" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 25 - Biomedicines">25</a></span>]. In this study, we focused on evaluating the impact of ionizable lipid and sterol substitutions while keeping other components constant to enable direct comparison across formulations. We selected three clinically approved ionizable lipids, ALC-0315 (ALC), SM-102 (SM), and DLin-MC3-DMA (MC3), alongside two well-characterized experimental lipids, C12-200 (C12) and DLin-KC2-DMA (KC2). Four sterols were tested: cholesterol (CHOL), the conventional sterol as control, β-stigmasterol (STIG), β-sitosterol (SITO), and γ-oryzanol (ORY). The helper lipid DSPC and the PEG-lipid 1,2-distearoyl-<i>sn</i>-glycero-3-phosphoethanolamine-<i>N</i>-[methoxy(polyethylene glycol)-2000] (DSPE-PEG2000) were used in all formulations at a fixed molar ratio (52.0:38.5:8.0:1.5 ionizable lipid: sterol: DSPC: DSPE-PEG2000). DSPE-PEG2000 was chosen over the shorter-chains 1,2-dimyristoyl-<i>sn</i>-glycero-3-methoxypolyethylene glycol-2000 (DMG-PEG2000) due to its improved anchoring stability and slower PEG desorption from the LNP surface, a property that is more suitable for therapeutic applications requiring extended systemic exposure, in contrast to the transient delivery kinetics preferred in vaccine settings. Pre-formulation lipid mixtures were prepared using simple thin film–hydration, followed by sonication, a robust and scalable method that facilitates size reduction and uniform lipid dispersion while enabling rapid screening of multiple formulations. Dynamic light scattering (DLS) analysis confirmed that all pre-formulation lipid mixtures had hydrodynamic diameters in the 100–300&nbsp;nm range, polydispersity between 0.1 and 0.4, and near-neutral zeta potentials (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S1</a>).</p>
            
            <p>Our initial goal was to investigate if the different pre-formulation lipid mixtures affect biocompatibility. We first assessed cell viability across a range of concentrations (10, 20, 45, 85, and 170&nbsp;µg mL<sup>−1</sup>) in Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cells and human embryonic kidney (HEK293T) cells (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll73095-fig-0001">1a,b</a>). The selected concentration range reflects both intended and elevated doses relevant to in vitro transfection and in vivo studies and is comparable to ranges used in prior cytocompatibility studies [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0026" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0026_R_d140344236e662" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 26 - Nature Communications">26</a>, <a href="#smll73095-bib-0027" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0027_R_d140344236e662" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 27 - Nature Communications">27</a></span>]. The highest concentration tested exceeds typical LNP doses reported for siRNA- or mRNA-mediated gene modulation in mammalian cells by approximately 6–30-fold [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0028" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0028_R_d140344236e665" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 28 - Nature Biotechnology">28</a>-<a href="#smll73095-bib-0030" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0030_R_d140344236e665" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 30 - Small">30</a></span>]. MTT assays in CHO cells (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll73095-fig-0001">1a</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2</a>) revealed a concentration-dependent decline in viability for most pre-formulation lipid mixtures. Strikingly, within each ionizable lipid series, pre-formulation lipid mixtures incorporating γ-oryzanol (ORY) consistently exhibited minimal cytotoxicity across all tested concentrations. In contrast, sterol variants containing CHOL, STIG and SITO showed significantly reduced cell viability, even at lower doses. Formulations containing KC2, SM, and MC3 as their ionizable lipids generally demonstrated enhanced biocompatibility, exhibiting better tolerance in treated cells. These trends were similarly observed in HEK293T cells (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#smll73095-fig-0001">1b</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S3</a>), confirming the robustness of the findings across cell types.</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="smll73095-fig-0001"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/bcf94c24-31af-4e52-a7ea-a449d088fb59/smll73095-fig-0001-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/bcf94c24-31af-4e52-a7ea-a449d088fb59/smll73095-fig-0001-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/7c69fd3c-8db8-4d5b-8b42-840152527ab5/smll73095-fig-0001-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/bcf94c24-31af-4e52-a7ea-a449d088fb59/smll73095-fig-0001-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">FIGURE 1<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=smll73095-fig-0001&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fsmll.202511946" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Pre-formulation lipid screening via cytocompatibility. MTT cell viability assays using (a) CHO and (b) HEK293T cells following 24&nbsp;h exposure to 20 different pre-formulation lipid mixtures across different concentration ranges (<i>n</i> = 3 independent experiments). Values are expressed as a percentage relative to the PBS control (0&nbsp;µg mL<sup>−1</sup>, 100% viability). Two-way ANOVA with Geisser-Greenhouse correction was used to evaluate the main effects of sterol type and concentration, as well as their interaction. Post hoc analysis was performed using Tukey's multiple comparisons test. Statistical comparisons reflect differences between ORY formulations with other sterol types across concentrations: *<i>p</i> &lt; 0.05, **<i>p</i> &lt; 0.01, ***<i>p</i> &lt; 0.001, ****<i>p</i> &lt; 0.0001. Complete MTT assay results with statistical comparisons are available in Figures <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2</a> and <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S3</a>.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>This consistently superior cytocompatibility observed in ORY-based lipid mixtures may be attributed to the intrinsic antioxidant properties of ORY, which has been shown to reduce intracellular reactive oxygen species (ROS) and inhibit ROS-mediated apoptotic pathways, thereby supporting cell survival and proliferation [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0022" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0022_R_d140344236e723" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 22 - Journal of Agricultural and Food Chemistry">22</a></span>]. Meanwhile, CHOL-based lipid mixtures demonstrated notable cytotoxicity at higher concentrations, likely due to their known potential to disrupt membrane integrity and interfere with cellular metabolic functions [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0031" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0031_R_d140344236e726" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 31 - Journal of Clinical Investigation">31</a></span>]. Similarly, SITO has been reported to accumulate in cell membranes, disrupt extracellular signaling and induce cell death [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0032" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0032_R_d140344236e729" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 32 - Journal of Medicinal Food">32</a></span>]. STIG, while structurally similar, has been shown to activate both apoptosis and autophagy by inhibiting the Akt/mTOR pathway. Chronic accumulation of STIG has also been linked to cardiovascular pathologies including left ventricular dysfunction, myocardial fibrosis, macrophage infiltration, and increased mortality [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0033" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0033_R_d140344236e732" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 33 - Communications Biology">33</a></span>], characteristics that raise concerns about its suitability for therapeutic applications.</p>
            
            <p>The observed differences in cytocompatibility between sterol variants are consistent with known sterol-dependent biological effects reported for lipid-based nanocarrier systems. Prior work in cholesterol-rich liposomal systems provides important mechanistic context for interpreting the biological effects of sterol composition observed here. For example, several studies have demonstrated that cholesterol-containing liposomes modulate immune environments [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0016" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0016_R_d140344236e738" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 16 - Journal of Controlled Release">16</a>, <a href="#smll73095-bib-0034" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0034_R_d140344236e738" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 34 - Journal for ImmunoTherapy of Cancer">34</a></span>], increased secretion of pro-inflammatory cytokines [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0015" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0015_R_d140344236e741" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 15 - Journal of Lipid Research">15</a></span>], enhanced macrophage activation and induce foam cell–like phenotypes in macrophages [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0017" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0017_R_d140344236e744" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 17 - ACS Nano">17</a></span>]. These studies highlight the potential for sterol chemistry to influence biological responses beyond nanoparticle delivery efficiency.</p>
            
            <p>Although ionizable lipid-based LNPs do not form classical phospholipid bilayers, both liposomes and LNPs typically incorporate comparable cholesterol molar fractions (approximately 30–50 mol%) [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0035" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0035_R_d140344236e751" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 35 - J Immunol Baltim Md 1950">35</a>-<a href="#smll73095-bib-0038" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0038_R_d140344236e751" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 38">38</a></span>] and are processed through similar endo-lysosomal pathways following cellular uptake [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0017" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0017_R_d140344236e754" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 17 - ACS Nano">17</a>, <a href="#smll73095-bib-0039" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0039_R_d140344236e754" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 39 - Biomaterials">39</a>, <a href="#smll73095-bib-0040" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0040_R_d140344236e754" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 40 - Nature Communications">40</a></span>]. Upon nanoparticle disassembly, cholesterol enters endogenous cellular lipid-handling pathways, where it may accumulate or be metabolized into oxysterols with recognized immunomodulatory activity, particularly in macrophages [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0017" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0017_R_d140344236e757" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 17 - ACS Nano">17</a></span>]. While direct evidence for cholesterol-driven immunomodulation in LNPs remains more limited than in liposomal systems, these shared features support the use of liposome studies as mechanistic references for conserved aspects of cholesterol handling rather than as structural analogues [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0041" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0041_R_d140344236e760" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 41 - ACS Nano">41</a></span>]. These considerations are especially relevant in cardiovascular disease contexts, where macrophage lipid loading, oxidative stress, and inflammatory signaling play central roles in disease progression [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0042" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0042_R_d140344236e763" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 42 - Signal Transduction and Targeted Therapy">42</a></span>], providing motivation for exploring alternative sterols such as γ-oryzanol in LNP formulations.</p>
            
            <p>Given that the lipid mixtures were designed for systemic delivery inevitably interact with blood components, we next assessed hemocompatibility across the same concentration range used in cytotoxicity assays. The pre-formulation lipid mixtures were incubated with freshly collected human whole blood, and hemolytic potential was evaluated by (1) measuring hemoglobin release spectrophotometrically as an indicator of red blood cell (RBC) hemolysis, and (2) performing blood counts using a Sysmex automated hematology analyzer (Figures <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S4–S8</a>). Hemolysis levels below 5% are generally considered acceptable for nanocarriers in systemic applications [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0043" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0043_R_d140344236e772" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 43 - Heliyon">43</a></span>]. Across all particles and concentrations tested (Figures <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S4–S8</a>), no substantial hemoglobin release was detected, and RBC, white blood cell (WBC), and platelet counts remained unchanged. These findings indicate that the tested pre-formulation lipid mixtures are hemo-compatible and support their favorable safety profile for systemic therapeutic use.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="smll73095-sec-0040">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="smll73095-sec-0040-title">2.2 γ-Oryzanol Formulations Show Enhanced Transfection Efficiencies</h3>
            
            <p>Based on the cytocompatibility and hemocompatibility profiles, pre-formulation lipid mixtures that maintained ≥ 75% viability across the tested concentrations were prioritized for further development. Among the five sterol-ionizable lipid combinations assessed, KC-ORY and SM-ORY were advanced because they displayed significantly improved tolerability relative to cholesterol and other phytosterols, while also exhibiting behavior distinct from the conventional cholesterol system and from each other. This aligned with the central objective of the study, which was to explore sterol chemistries that introduce new biological and structural profiles rather than simply replicate established formulations. STIG- and SITO-containing variants did not meet the ≥ 75% viability requirement and were therefore not advanced, consistent with reports describing less favorable biological responses associated with these sterols [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0032" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0032_R_d140344236e787" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 32 - Journal of Medicinal Food">32</a>, <a href="#smll73095-bib-0033" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0033_R_d140344236e787" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 33 - Communications Biology">33</a></span>]. To our knowledge, ORY-containing LNPs have not previously been reported for mRNA delivery. Cholesterol formulations, KC-CHOL and SM-CHOL, were therefore retained as reference benchmarks given their established role in clinically used nucleic acid nanomedicines [<span><a href="#smll73095-bib-0008" class="bibLink tab-link" id="smll73095-bib-0008_R_d140344236e790" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 8 - Journal of Pharmaceutical Sciences">8</a></span>].</p>
            
            <p>In the following stage, LNP formulations derived from the selected pre-formulation lipid mixtures were prepared using microfluidic mixing with a NanoAssemblr Ignite, enabling precise and rapid LNP assembly. The resulting “proto-particle” dispersions were dialyzed against PBS, yielding the final LNPs for downstream testing. Using a representative formulation KC2 CHOL, we confirmed that increasing the total flow rate (8, 10, and 12&nbsp;mL min<sup>−1</sup>) with a fixed flow ratio (3:1 aqueous-to-ethanolic) results in a reduction in particle size (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S9</a>), providing a foundation for controlled size tuning. To systematically incorporate size as a variable, flow rates of 8 and 12&nbsp;mL min<sup>−1</sup> were selected to produce two distinct particle sizes per formulation. For clarity, formulations are hereafter designated by the identity of the ionizable lipid, the sterol, and the fabrication flow rate (Table&nbsp;<a class="tableLink scrollableLink" title="Link to table" href="#smll73095-tbl-0001">1</a>). SM with ORY (SO) LNPs were excluded from this stage due to poor performance in the initial transfection trials.</p>

               <div class="article-section__table-footnotes">
                  <ul>
                     <li id="smll73095-tbl1-note-0001" class="footNotePopup__item" title="Footnote 1"><span class="number"><sup>*</sup> </span>KC2 = Dlin-KC2-DMA; SM = SM102.</li>
